As built: 2,795,520 bytes at the commit that applied it: 307,712 over Phase 4, 300.5 KiB, 50,688 bytes (49.5 KiB) under the 350 KiB (358,400-byte) gate, and 350,208 under the 3 MB budget. Past 350 KiB, this decision is reopened again.
21. **A live test may set `sqlh_test`'s compatibility level (owner decision, 2026-10-18, for R37).** Only `sqlh_test`, the scratch database, and only after the `dbo.sqlh_scratch` marker check; the test reads the level first and arms the cleanup that restores it (`ALTER DATABASE ... SET COMPATIBILITY_LEVEL`) before it changes it (R24), so a failed or killed test leaves the database as it found it, or the next run's cleanup puts it back. No other database-level setting, and no other database. **As built (2026-10-18, `2504b7c`, `a32859e`; the decision is unchanged):** the level is saved in the tests' own table `dbo.probe_compatibility_level`, the restore armed first, and kept until the level is set back; every level batch starts with a server-side check that refuses all but `sqlh_test` with its marker; the level test runs alone, under an exclusive file lock every other live test shares; and "the next run's cleanup" is a start-of-run repair: once per test process, before any live test shares the lock, a saved level a killed run left is put back, or every live test in that process fails with the reason (plan-history.md, Phase 5.2 and 5.3 review fixes, Decision 21, as built, with its two limits). Why: R37's acceptance needs SQL Server itself to run `describe`'s aggregation at levels 110 and 120 with a name holding an XML-invalid character, and no other database on the test server runs at those levels; a one-off manual check was the alternative, but a test keeps the threshold from regressing.
22. **Kinds of change are granted separately, per profile and per run (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D1(b)).** Changes come in six kinds a person can grant, named in DBA words: `data`, `maintenance`, `tuning`, `kill`, `remote` and `unvetted` (code sqlh can't classify). A profile's `allow` list, written by the owner, says which kinds it permits, and it holds even under `write_policy = "deny"`; a run uses a listed kind with `--allow KIND`. `--allow-write` stays as the blanket switch for everything outside the deny tier. `read`, `scratch` and the deny tier are never switch names; finer reason labels (contention, settings, cost, ...) appear only in refusal text and the docs. Why: today a `deny` profile, every new profile's (decision 12), has no way to update statistics, force a plan or kill a runaway session short of moving to `flag`, which opens data writes too; separate kinds let an owner grant the narrow change without the broad one. Retiring `--allow-write` (option (c)) was not chosen: it would break existing scripts and agents' habits for little gain. The mechanics (the `allow` list's defaults by `write_policy`, `--allow` limited to it, which kinds keep the rollback wrapper) are D11; the deny tier is D10; the `execute` tool is D15. Like `deny`, the list is an accident guard: an agent that can run `profile add` can change it (R19).
23. **Refusals say what they are, and what lifts them (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D2(a)).** Now, independent of classifier v2: an unknown procedure is refused as `EXEC X isn't on sqlh's list of read-only procedures, so sqlh can't tell what it does` (implicit calls: `... it isn't on sqlh's list of read-only procedures`), with `Rerun with --allow-write to run it as written`; an unknown DBCC command as `DBCC X isn't on sqlh's list of read-only DBCC commands`, naming its documented replacement first where there is one (`PAGE` → `sys.dm_db_page_info`, `LOGINFO` → `sys.dm_db_log_info`, ...); DDL refusals name the statement (`ALTER EVENT SESSION`, not a bare `ALTER`); `sp_query_store_flush_db` gets the aggregation advice in place of a flag. With v2: every refusal names its kind or reason label (decision 22) and the narrowest switch that lifts it, or says that none does (the deny tier). No JSON `kind` member for now (contract 2.2, if a consumer needs it). Why: "would write" is wrong for a procedure or command sqlh merely doesn't know, and steers agents to `--allow-write` for reads. It changes the output contract: DESIGN.md §5.1 and §7, the golden tests and README's "Built for agents" change with the code.
24. **Read-only runs and `sqlh mcp` refuse lock-holding SQL; SNAPSHOT becomes a run setting after a live test (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D3(a) and (i)).** Refused, and lifted by the `data` kind (decision 22): the hints `UPDLOCK`, `XLOCK`, `TABLOCKX` and `HOLDLOCK`; `SERIALIZABLE` and `REPEATABLEREAD` (hint or isolation level) and `REPEATABLE READ` after `ISOLATION LEVEL`; `SET DEADLOCK_PRIORITY` with anything but `LOW` or a literal from −10 to −5; `SET LOCK_TIMEOUT` outside a literal from 0 to the run's own lock timeout (10,000 ms when the run has none; never −1 or a variable); with `WAITFOR`, `sp_getapplock` and `sp_releaseapplock`, refused already. `TABLOCK` alone, `NOLOCK`, `READPAST` and the other hints stay reads. The advice names what the caller can change: remove the hint; `--lock-timeout`; in MCP, the profile's `lock_timeout`. Why: inside the wrapper these hold their locks to the run's final rollback, the one risk the wrapper makes worse, and the in-SQL `SET`s override decision 7 from inside the input; the rare legitimate SERIALIZABLE read gives way. **SNAPSHOT:** a run setting like decision 7's READ UNCOMMITTED (`--snapshot`, a profile's `snapshot = true`), set in the session setup before `BEGIN TRANSACTION`, exclusive with `read_uncommitted`, once a live test shows it works inside the wrapper; then an in-SQL `SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SNAPSHOT` is refused with advice naming it. The test needs `ALLOW_SNAPSHOT_ISOLATION ON` on a test database, a database-level change outside decision 21, so it waits for the owner's approval of that step.
25. **Linked-server reads are allowed with a note; paths an agent chose are refused (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D4a(i) and D4b(i)).** **D4a:** a four-part read through a linked server a DBA configured (`SELECT ... FROM Lnk.db.sys.x`) is a read in read-only runs and `sqlh mcp`, with a note naming the linked server and saying the rollback doesn't reach it; its login mapping is a DBA's grant, so permissions decide. `OPENQUERY`, `OPENROWSET`, `OPENDATASOURCE` and `EXEC ... AT` stay refused. Final once the MSDTC question is answered (whether a four-part read inside the wrapper runs, promotes to a distributed transaction, or fails; command-categories.md §10); that test has not run. **D4b:** in the path-taking functions (`fn_xe_file_target_read_file`, `fn_get_audit_file`, `fn_trace_gettable`, `dm_os_enumerate_filesystem`, `dm_os_file_exists`), a UNC, URL, device, variable or expression path is `remote` and refused, matched in every spelling of the name; any other `fn_`/`dm_` function with a non-local literal fails closed; `fn_dump_dblog` is `unvetted`. The docs teach `system_health*.xel`, and a variable path gets two-step advice (read the path, pass it as a literal). A sqlh-owned read of the server's own configured audit and XE paths may follow if audits on shares prove common. Why: a path an agent chose makes the service account authenticate to a host the agent named, and an agent misled by text it read can name any host.
26. **Read list round 2, the exact-shape reads, and EXECUTE AS reads in the CLI (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D5, all of (a)–(e), with (a1)).** Each entry ships only after its live check inside the wrapper (command-categories.md §10). **(a)** Round 2's argument-checked reads: `DBCC SQLPERF(LOGSPACE)`, `DBCC CHECKIDENT (<name>, NORESEED)`, `DBCC CHECKCONSTRAINTS` naming a table or constraint, `sp_autostats` in its display form, `sp_spaceused` with `@updateusage` the literal `'false'`, `sp_helplogins`, `sp_helpsrvrole`, `sp_helpsrvrolemember`, `sp_helpntgroup`, `sp_srvrolepermission`, `sp_dbfixedrolepermission`, `sp_change_users_login 'Report'`, `sp_linkedservers`, `sp_helplinkedsrvlogin`, `sp_helpserver`; the msdb rule (`sp_help_job`, `sp_help_jobhistory`, `sp_help_jobstep`, `sp_help_jobactivity`, `sp_help_operator`, `sp_help_alert`, `sysmail_help_status_sp`, `sysmail_help_queue_sp`, qualified exactly `msdb.dbo.`); the scratch additions (`UPDATE STATISTICS #t`, `CREATE STATISTICS ... ON #t`, `ALTER INDEX ... ON #t`, `MERGE INTO #t`). **(a1)** The one-shot `sp_server_diagnostics` (no repeat interval, or `0`) is allowed in `sqlh mcp` too, a bounded server-side wait of about 5 s inside the call's timeout, once a live test has measured it; its repeat form is refused everywhere. **(b)** `DBCC CHECKDB`/`CHECKTABLE ... WITH ESTIMATEONLY` and `sp_estimate_data_compression_savings` stay `maintenance`: neither is a cheap read. **(c)** `DBCC MEMORYSTATUS` is on the list; the docs and the skill teach `sys.dm_os_memory_clerks`, `sys.dm_os_process_memory` and `sys.dm_os_sys_memory` first, since it returns many result sets. **(d)** Exact-shape reads run alone and without the wrapper when the input is exactly one of them: `RESTORE HEADERONLY|FILELISTONLY|LABELONLY FROM DISK = N'<local literal>'` (optionally `WITH FILE = <int>`) and `KILL <int> WITH STATUSONLY`; the classifier still runs, permissions are the boundary, and the class is built only once live checks show they fail inside the wrapper and work outside it. **(e)** `EXECUTE AS USER|LOGIN = N'<literal>'`, without `NO REVERT` or `COOKIE`, is allowed in CLI read-only runs, inside the wrapper with the classifier on, so a DBA can ask "can user X read this"; `sqlh mcp` refuses it until a reset test shows the reset reverts it. `IMPERSONATE` is the boundary, and the reads run with the impersonated principal's rights. The doc fix rides along: DESIGN.md §7 lists the `sp_help*` names it admits rather than implying a prefix.
27. **A client-side `:pause N` between batches, capped at 60 s (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D6(a)).** A line `:pause N` (whole seconds, 1 to 60) ends the batch before it, like `GO`; sqlh sends nothing for N seconds, then runs the next batch on the same connection, in the CLI's input and in an MCP call's `sql`. The batches share the session and its `#temp` tables, so an agent snapshots any DMV, pauses and computes the delta in SQL. Bounds: each pause and the sum of a run's or call's pauses at most 60 s, at most `-t` when `-t` isn't 0, and in MCP at most the call's timeout; past a bound it is a usage error before anything runs (exit 3); `--check` skips pauses; cancellation during a pause is immediate. Output: one line `-- paused N s` where the pause was. `WAITFOR` stays refused in every read-only run and every `sqlh mcp` call, so MCP's rule on waits is untouched. Why: a client-side pause holds no server worker, works in both front ends and serves any DMV, where `WAITFOR` holds a worker and the refusal pushed agents to CPU spin loops that distort the waits and CPU they measure. It depends on decision 24: with lock-holding SQL refused, the idle wrapper across a pause holds only the session's `#temp` objects and an open transaction. Presets (`sqlh sample waits|io|counters`) may follow on top.
28. **sqlh ships its own `activity` and `errorlog` reads, and `info` reports the login's view permissions (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D7(a)).** Both are sqlh's own SQL (exempt from the classifier, always read-only, inside the wrapper), as CLI commands and MCP tools, like the schema commands. **`activity`** (`--plans` optional): one row per user session that is running, blocked, blocking, or sleeping with an open transaction, with its blocker, a head-blocker flag and chain depth, waits, resource use, login, host, `program_name`, database, command and statement, and the input buffer for head blockers and sleeping sessions with an open transaction. It hides only its own `@@SPID` and, in `sqlh mcp`, that server's idle pooled connections; a busy pooled session shows, marked as the server's own. Without `VIEW SERVER PERFORMANCE STATE` (`VIEW SERVER STATE` before 2022) it says the login sees only its own session. **`errorlog`** (`--since`, `--search`, `--agent`, `--archive`): newest first, so the row limit shows the end of the log that matters; `xp_readerrorlog` with a server-side window where the login may run it, else `INSERT ... EXEC sys.sp_readerrorlog` sorted in SQL (its working inside the wrapper for a least-privilege login is a live check still to run). **`info`** lists the login's view permissions in one line (`VIEW SERVER PERFORMANCE STATE`, `VIEW SERVER STATE`, `VIEW ANY ERROR LOG`, `VIEW ANY DEFINITION`, `VIEW SERVER SECURITY STATE`, sysadmin). Why: MCP is the agents' main path (agent trial 3), and these give it an `sp_WhoIsActive` substitute and a usable error log with no trust question; `sp_readerrorlog` alone returns the log oldest first, so the default row limit hid an incident's lines behind startup messages. Cost: new output shapes (golden tests, DESIGN.md §5 and §9, README), and MCP size against decision 20's 350 KiB gate (310.0 KiB used) and the exe's 3 MB budget.
29. **Showplan hygiene in Phase 6; the plan modes after classifier v2 (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D8(a)).** In Phase 6: when the input mentions `SHOWPLAN_XML`, `SHOWPLAN_ALL`, `SHOWPLAN_TEXT`, `FMTONLY`, `PARSEONLY` or (outside `--check`) `NOEXEC`, sqlh sends the matching `SET ... OFF` before its closing `ROLLBACK`, each `SHOWPLAN_*` as a request of its own, so the rollback runs and `sqlh mcp` keeps the connection; and a batch that holds `SET SHOWPLAN_*` with any other statement is refused locally (`... must be alone in its batch; ... Put GO after SET SHOWPLAN_XML ON`). After classifier v2, as their own task: an estimated-plan mode (`--plan`, an MCP `plan` tool) for reads, then estimated plans of writes and procedures once a live proof shows nothing runs under SHOWPLAN (a `deny` profile may plan writes, since nothing runs), an actual-plan mode, and the plan summary (DESIGN.md §13's M3 `explain`). Why: hygiene is cheap and ends MCP's dropped connection; the modes are output-contract work with golden tests.
30. **Five small Part 1 items, all accepted (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D9(a)–(e)).** **(a)** `NOLOCK` or `READUNCOMMITTED` as a hint, or `SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL READ UNCOMMITTED` in the SQL, gives the run decision 7's honesty note, worded for its source (`-- note: some reads were uncommitted (NOLOCK in the SQL): rows may include uncommitted, duplicated or missing data`); they are not refused. **(b)** Host-disclosure reads stay allowed (`xp_instance_regread`, `sys.dm_server_services`, drives, `xp_msver`), documented in DESIGN.md §7 as a disclosure; a DBA stops `xp_instance_regread` by revoking `EXECUTE` from public. **(c)** The login's application name says what the run may do: `sqlh` (CLI read-only), `sqlh mcp` (MCP read tools), `sqlh (writes)` (`--allow-write` or `write_policy = "allow"`), `sqlh (allow: K,...)` (kind grants); a client-asserted courtesy label, fixed at login, so a pooled MCP connection keeps `sqlh mcp` and the `execute` tool needs connections of its own. **(d)** `sys.dm_db_index_physical_stats` with the literal `NULL` or `DEFAULT` as its first argument and the literal `'SAMPLED'` or `'DETAILED'` as its fifth is refused, with advice to name the database and object or use `'LIMITED'`; a variable mode can't be seen and is documented. **Amended (owner decision, 2026-11-02: "yes, add them all"):** the refusal also takes the literals `0`, `'0'`, `''` and `N''` as the database, and a binary literal as the mode: decoded as UTF-16 LE and refused only when it reads as `DETAILED` or `SAMPLED`, so `N'LIMITED'` written in binary stays allowed. Any other literal measured to act the same way as the database is the same rule; an expression stays out (DESIGN.md §7), and that includes a sign on anything but a number (`+''`, `+NULL`, `+'DETAILED'`; owner, 2026-11-02: "leave them out and documented"): nobody writes one by accident, and `'' + ''` and `0 + 0` pass either way. **(e)** `sqlh mcp` refuses `SET AUTOPILOT`, `OPEN SYMMETRIC KEY`, `OPEN MASTER KEY` and `SET IDENTITY_INSERT` until a reset test shows the reset clears them, with advice to run them with the CLI, whose session ends with the run; MCP loses `DECRYPTBYKEY` reads and `SET IDENTITY_INSERT #t` until then.
31. **A deny tier, refused in every mode, `--allow-write` included (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D10(b) and (e)).** A lexical pass that runs before anything connects, in every mode, with bounded lookahead (linear, no "too complex" refusal). Members: `RESTORE DATABASE` and `RESTORE LOG` in every form; `BACKUP` of any kind to anything but local literals (a non-local `DISK|TAPE|FILE` literal, `URL`, a variable, a logical device); `SHUTDOWN`; `xp_cmdshell` and the `sp_OA*` procedures under any qualification; `DBCC WRITEPAGE` and `DBCC CHECK*` with `REPAIR_*`; `ABORT_AFTER_WAIT = BLOCKERS` anywhere; `ALTER`/`DROP EVENT SESSION` of `system_health`; `ALTER DATABASE ... SET QUERY_STORE CLEAR`; `sp_query_store_set_hints` with `ABORT_QUERY_EXECUTION` or a non-literal hint; `EXECUTE AS ... WITH NO REVERT`; `DROP DATABASE`; and `sp_configure` with a value whose option is any part of `xp_cmdshell`, `Ole Automation Procedures` or `clr enabled`, holds `%`, `_` or `[`, or is not a literal (`'cmdshell', 1` passes `--allow-write` today). **(e):** once the one-literal rule of D13(b) exists, the deny tier also reads one literal of dynamic SQL in every mode (`EXEC (N'...')`, `sp_executesql`'s statement), which catches `EXEC ('xp_cmdshell ''dir''')`. Worded as an accident guard: the refusal says only that no switch lifts it (`... is on sqlh's deny list (<why>); nothing was run. No switch runs it; ask a person to run it outside sqlh if it is intended`), and DESIGN.md §7 says what gets past it (a variable or concatenated dynamic SQL, `EXEC @var`, a synonym, a wrapper procedure); the login's permissions are the boundary. Not added: the candidates (`ALTER DATABASE ... SET OFFLINE|EMERGENCY|SINGLE_USER`, `DBCC SHRINKDATABASE`, the instance-wide cache clears, faked statistics) stay where the design puts them ("other changes", or `maintenance` for `SHRINKDATABASE`), to revisit if asked; no profile-extendable list (D10(d)) and no refusal of dynamic SQL under `--allow-write` (D10(c)).
32. **Grants: a profile `allow` list and a per-run `--allow`, with the wrapper per kind (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D11(a) and (i)).** A profile's owner-written `allow` list (a TOML array of kind names, `allow = ["maintenance", "tuning"]`) is honored under every `write_policy`, `deny` included, so `deny` keeps meaning "no data or schema writes, no blanket switch". Absent the key: every kind under `flag` and `allow`, none under `deny`; an explicit list replaces the default; a run without a profile is `flag`. A run lifts exactly the kinds it names with `--allow KIND[,KIND]`, and only listed ones: an unlisted kind is a usage error (exit 3), `--allow kill: profile P doesn't allow kill (its allow list is maintenance, tuning)`; `--allow` with `--allow-write` is a usage error; under `write_policy = "allow"`, a run without `--allow` keeps today's behavior and a run with `--allow K` gets only K. The wrapper: a run keeps it only if every granted kind keeps it (`unvetted` and `remote` keep it; `data`, `maintenance`, `tuning` and `kill` drop it); `--allow unvetted` with a kind that drops it is a usage error; without the wrapper, a write to a `#x` the input doesn't create is refused (the alias case the wrapper covered), these runs count hidden rows exactly (decision 6), and `--read-uncommitted` is a usage error (decision 7). The `sqlh mcp` read tools ignore the list (decision 16). A local `BACKUP ... TO DISK = N'<local literal>' WITH COPY_ONLY` is `maintenance`; any other backup is "other changes" (`--allow-write`), and the first `execute` tool leaves the copy out. Like `deny`, the list is an accident guard (R19). `--help` gains an `--allow` line within its 62-line test.
33. **Kill is a kind of its own (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D12(a)).** `kill` admits `KILL <integer literal>` (a session id) and `msdb.dbo.sp_stop_job`; other `KILL` forms (a UOW GUID, `STATS JOB`, `QUERY NOTIFICATION SUBSCRIPTION`) stay "other changes", and `KILL n WITH STATUSONLY` alone is an exact-shape read (decision 26). Its runs drop the wrapper, since `KILL` can't run inside a user transaction (Msg 6115). `ALTER ANY CONNECTION` stays the boundary. A `deny` profile that lists `kill` can end a runaway session without opening data writes. Never through MCP's read tools; through `execute` only where listed (D15). Session ids are reused, so once agents use `kill`, a sqlh-built `sqlh kill N --login-time T` follows, which checks in the same batch that the session is the one named before it kills it.
34. **One literal of dynamic SQL is classified, and sqlh runs a batch per database itself (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D13(b) and (d)).** **(b):** `EXEC sp_executesql N'...'` (its statement, named `@stmt` or first positional) and `EXEC (N'...')` with no concatenation, `AT` or `AS` are classified as their own batch, after unescaping `''`, under the caller's whole policy (the mode, or the run's granted kinds), so `WAITFOR`, app locks and session context stay out of every MCP call however they are wrapped; one level deep (dynamic SQL inside the literal is `unvetted`); the literal's bytes count against the batch's work budget; a refusal inside it is reported at the literal's line. A variable or concatenated string stays `unvetted`. The deny tier reuses this rule (decision 31(e)). Tests named in command-categories.md §7.6. **(d):** CLI `--each-db [PATTERN]` and an MCP `databases` argument (names or one glob): sqlh lists, in its own SQL, the online databases the login can open, then runs the same classified batches after `USE [db]` for each, on one connection, inside the one wrapper; one result set with a leading `database` column when every database's result has the same columns, labeled result sets otherwise; an error in one database is labeled and the run goes on (exit 1 if any failed); `-t` per batch per database. An output-contract change (DESIGN.md §5, golden tests). **(c) dropped:** `sp_MSforeachdb` and `sp_MSforeachtable` stay `unvetted` (undocumented, and known to skip databases). Neither (b) nor (d) needs a kind, so both may be built with Part 1.
35. **Owner-written trusted entries for community procedures (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D14(b)).** A profile key `trusted_procedures`, written by the owner; no built-in trust, and starter entries go in the docs, since the procedures' parameters change often and a stale built-in list would fail open. Each entry: an exact three-part name (other spellings refused with advice to use it); an allowlist of named parameters (positional ones refused); literal caps for parameters that wait, below `-t` or the call's timeout; the write parameters refused in every entry by exact name (`@destination_table`, FRK's `@OutputDatabaseName`, `@OutputSchemaName`, `@OutputTableName*`, `@OutputTableRetentionDays`, `@OutputServerName`, `@LogMessage`, `@log_to_table` and the other `@log_*`, `@LogToTable`, `@Email*`, `@PushBackupHistoryToListener`, `@WriteBackupsTo*`, `@keep_alive`, `@cleanup`, and `sp_Blitz`'s `@CheckServerInfo = 1`), never a prefix such as `@Output*`; and an optional pinned SHA2-256 hash of the module's definition. Before each call sqlh sends, in the same batch, a check that refuses a missing or invisible module, `EXECUTE AS` other than CALLER, any certificate signature (`sys.crypt_properties`, read at every call), and, with a pin, a changed or unreadable definition (a pin needs `VIEW DEFINITION`). The call runs inside the wrapper, in CLI read-only runs and in `sqlh mcp`; in MCP, until a reset test shows the reset clears session-owned app locks, session context and open keys, sqlh closes the connection after every trusted call instead of pooling it (one login on the next call). Never trusted: `sp_HumanEvents` (it creates event sessions) and Ola Hallengren's `IndexOptimize`, `DatabaseIntegrityCheck` and `DatabaseBackup` (they refuse to run in a transaction). Documented gaps: the check and the call are separate statements (time of check vs use), the hash covers only the named module, and whether a login with only `EXECUTE` sees the module's `sys.crypt_properties` rows is a live check still to run (if not, an entry requires `VIEW DEFINITION`). It needs no kind, so it may be built with Part 1.
36. **No `execute` tool in Phase 6 (owner decision, 2026-10-23, Phase 6, command-categories.md D15(a)).** `sqlh mcp` stays read-only, as decision 16 set it: every change, whatever its kind, goes through the CLI, with the profile's `allow` list, `--allow KIND` or `--allow-write`, and the deny tier (decisions 22, 31, 32). The recommendation was a first increment for listed `tuning`, `kill` and `maintenance` behind `--execute`; it was not taken. Decision 16's reason stands: under an `execute` tool the last check is the harness's approval prompt, which users often set to always allow, while a CLI change shows its kinds on its own command line. The design in command-categories.md §7.8 (offered only with `--execute` and an explicit `allow` list, a required `kinds` argument, unpooled connections, the deny tier) is kept for a later phase, after an agent trial with the kinds in the CLI. Part 2 needs no MCP work as a result; D9(c)'s application name keeps `sqlh mcp` for every pooled connection.
37. **The profile commands are `sqlh profiles ...` (owner decision, 2026-10-26, from manual testing).** `sqlh profiles add|list|test|rm` manages profiles; `--profile NAME` uses one in a run. `sqlh profile ...`, the older spelling, still works and is no longer documented. Why: the owner typed `sqlh --profile add Dev ...` for `sqlh profile add Dev ...`, and the two spellings differed only by the dashes; the plural reads as "manage the set" and keeps one vocabulary with `profiles.toml` and `--profile` (a different word, such as `config`, was considered and not chosen, as a third term for the same thing). Since `641848c`, a `--profile` that names `add`, `list`, `test` or `rm` and isn't a saved profile is refused as that typo, naming the right command. Every message, `--help`, the README, DESIGN.md, mcp-mode.md and schema-commands.md use the new spelling; PLAN.md, plan-history.md and the notes keep the old one where they record what was run then.
38. **The profile commands write paths as typed (owner decision, 2026-10-26, from manual testing; an exception to decision 11).** In `sqlh profiles add|list|test|rm`, the profiles file's path in a note keeps single backslashes (`-- note: profiles file: C:\Users\...\profiles.toml`), with control characters and line breaks still escaped (`output::path_as_typed`, `Output::note_with_path`, text format only). Why: the doubled backslashes looked wrong in a Windows terminal, and these commands are setup a person runs, whose paths nothing decodes. Everywhere else decision 11 stands: query runs, the schema commands and `sqlh mcp` keep one escape rule for every field, so their output stays unambiguous. Writing raw paths everywhere was considered and not chosen.
39. **A stopped count says how to keep the rest of its batch (owner decision, 2026-10-31, from the owner's benchmark in a real environment).** A result capped by `--max-rows` still stops counting after the 250 ms budget and cancels the rest of its batch (decision 6, unchanged), so a multi-statement batch can lose its later results. The run's note now names the fix: `-- note: counting stopped in batch 1: the rest of that batch was cancelled, so its later statements did not run; put each query in its own batch (GO) to keep them; --exact-count counts every row` (in `sqlh mcp` too, whose `sql` takes `GO`). Counting such a batch exactly whenever the guard sees more than one statement was considered and not chosen: slower turns, and T-SQL's statement boundaries are only a lexical guess. The Phase 8 skill is to teach one query per batch. From the same benchmark, also fixed (`033f503`): `describe` on SQL Server 2016, which has no `STRING_AGG`; and `--help` says `--max-rows 0` is still within `--max-output`.
40. **Multi-subnet failover is always explicit: off unless `-M` (owner decision, 2026-10-31, driver upgrade, docs/notes/driver-0.3.0.md §1.1).** sqlh passes `Some(options.multi_subnet_failover)` to mssql-tds 0.3.0, never `None`, which would inherit the driver's default (on) or `MSSQL_RS_MULTISUBNET_FAILOVER_DEFAULT`. Connects stay sequential, with today's timings, exit codes and texts (connect-failures.md); `-M`'s 0.3.0 behaviour (local names connect over parallel TCP; `np:`, `lpc:`, `admin:`, LocalDB and a named instance without a port refused; a parallel timeout is exit 4) goes in DESIGN.md §4. *Superseded in part (2026-10-31, the internal review, accepted by the external review's judgment 2): a parallel race in which no address accepted TCP within `-l` is a connect failure, exit 2, `no address accepted a TCP connection within N s (-M tries them all at once)`; a login that stalls after TCP stays exit 4 at `-l` + 1 s (DESIGN.md §4, driver-0.3.0.md §8).* Tests: the policy a connect resolves to, and the environment variable set to `true` and to garbage leaving `-S 127.0.0.1,1` unchanged.
41. **Driver session recovery stays off (owner decision, 2026-10-31, driver upgrade, driver-0.3.0.md §2).** `connect_retry_count = 0` keeps the driver from advertising or using idle connection resiliency, in both 0.2.0 and 0.3.0, and the engine's one retry on a new connection (decision 19) stays the only reconnect, with honest connect times. A guard at connect checks that the client reports session recovery off, and a fake-server test checks that LOGIN7 carries no session recovery feature. Why: a silent driver reconnect would hide a login in the status line's times, bypass the engine's peer-drop rule, could add a second login to the engine's, and would continue an `--allow-write` script in a new session; it gains the read-only wrapper nothing, since recovery is refused inside a transaction.
42. **mssql-tds 0.3.0's DNS cache is accepted, documented (owner decision, 2026-10-31, driver upgrade, driver-0.3.0.md §1.2).** It can't be turned off; it matters only to `sqlh mcp`'s long-lived process, and only if the server acknowledges the feature (likely only Azure; the on-prem SQL Server 2025 test server answers `allowed=false`, measured 2026-10-31) and a later lookup of the name fails. DESIGN.md §8 says so, and upstream-issues.md U7 asks for an opt-out, for the owner to file.
43. **Server routing is accepted as the server's decision, and DESIGN.md §8's wording corrected (owner decision, 2026-10-31, driver upgrade, driver-0.3.0.md §1.3).** A server can route a login, and the driver follows with the same credentials (0.2.0 already did; 0.3.0's Enhanced Routing can also name the database). DESIGN.md §8 now says a stored password goes to its profile's own server "or to a server that server routes the login to"; R20's protection against an agent repointing a profile is unaffected. Fake-server tests pin the routing behaviour in step 2, and upstream-issues.md U8 asks for a way to refuse a route, for the owner to file.
44. **Versioning: the exe says which build it is, in `--version` and in its Windows properties (owner decision, 2026-10-31, from manual testing: Explorer's Properties > Details showed nothing, and `--help` didn't mention `--version`).** Built the same day; DESIGN.md §4 has the exact behaviour.
    - **The version resource:** `build.rs` writes a VERSIONINFO resource as a compiled .res file and links it, with no rc.exe and no crate, so it works wherever the exe builds. It adds 1,024 bytes and no import.
    - **The numeric version:** major.minor.patch, then the release workflow's run number (`SQLH_BUILD`; 0 for any other build), so the numbers only grow: `0.1.0-dev.5` is 0.1.0.5, and a 0.1.0 release built at run 9 is 0.1.0.9.
    - **The fields:** `CompanyName` `Tom Willwerth`, the copyright from LICENSE, product `sqlh`.
    - **`--help`:** its first line names `--version` and `--help`, with no new line.
    - **Local builds:** a build outside the release workflow reports `<Cargo.toml version>-local`, so it never passes for a release.
    - **Checks:** `the_exe_carries_its_version_in_its_windows_properties` reads the resource through Windows PowerShell 5.1, and `a_build_outside_the_release_workflow_is_marked_local`; both run in `check.ps1`, against the release exe too. The release workflow checks the signed exe's properties against `SQLH_VERSION` and `SQLH_BUILD`.
    - **Policy:** semver, with `Cargo.toml` as the source and a release tag that must match it. Before 1.0, a release that changes the output contract or flags bumps the minor, and a fix-only release bumps the patch. The JSON contract keeps its own version (DESIGN.md §5.2).
45. **A capped result no longer cancels later statements unseen; it gets a longer count first, and a run that still loses them says `partial` (owner decision, 2026-10-31, from MCP client feedback: docs/notes/mcp-client-feedback.md).** This amends decision 6.
    - **The problem:** a stopped count cancelled the rest of its batch while the status line still began `-- ok`. One client's model said it was the only issue that made it "confidently report something false".
    - **The link:** over the owner's real link the 250 ms budget stopped after about 600 wide rows, so it hit modest results, and models often send several queries in one call.
    - **The longer budget:** a batch that may produce output from more than one statement counts the hidden rows of a capped result for 5 s, not 250 ms, so its later statements still run. `guard::several_outputs` counts the statements conservatively: an `EXEC` counts as two; `DECLARE`, `SET` and `USE` count as none. A batch of one output statement keeps 250 ms, since a stop there cancels nothing.
    - **The run's result-to-statement mapping:** sqlh can't tell from the server's replies which statement a result came from, so a several-statement batch gets the longer budget for its last result too. It then waits up to 5 s for an exact count that cancels nothing.
    - **When even that stops the count,** whether by the 5 s or by `-t`: the status line begins `-- partial:` instead of `-- ok:`, exit 0, and names the batches whose rest didn't run (`partial: 1 batch, counting stopped in 1, the rest of batch 1 not run, ...`). The note says the same, with the `GO` advice.
    - **A stop in a single-output batch** keeps `ok` and its note says only that the total is a lower bound.
    - **JSON's contract is unchanged (2.1):** `summary` carries the status line, `ok` stays true for exit 0, and `counting_stopped` keeps its 2.0 meaning, that the rest of that batch may have been cancelled.
46. **The CLI is the center; MCP is a second front end on the same core (owner decision, 2026-11-01, from the review of the plan, design and vision).** sqlh is first a command-line tool for shell-driven agents, small-context models included: the owner's original vision, turn-by-turn speed for agents like Qwen. `sqlh mcp` serves MCP hosts from the same execution core. When the two compete for design, size budget or review attention, the CLI comes first: a feature is designed as a command or flag, and MCP gets it through the shared core.
    - **Why:** the CLI costs an agent's context least. `--help` is 4.7 KB (about 1,200 tokens), read once and only when needed; MCP's nine tool definitions and its instructions are 12.5 KB of JSON (roughly 3,000–4,000 tokens; `query` alone 2.2 KB), sent with every request of a session whether or not it touches SQL (measured 2026-11-01 on the release build of `7feae17`). After Phase 6 slice 2's follow-ups (2026-11-22, `7784761`, one profile listed): the eleven tools' `tools/list` result is 15,785 bytes and the `initialize` answer, with the instructions, 867, about 16.7 KB (roughly 4,000–5,300 tokens at the same ratio).
    - **MCP stays:** agent trial 3's model (`qwen3.8`, offered both) and the owner's Claude Desktop sessions (2026-10-31) used it, and it is the only surface with connection reuse today.
    - **Open, in this order:** (a) the scope of hosted MCP over Streamable HTTP: loopback, a team service (on an application server, which decision 1 as amended allows for MCP) or the internet (claude.ai's remote connectors connect from Anthropic's cloud); (b) whether MCP moves to an exe of its own (decision 20), which follows (a), so that `sqlh.exe` never gains a network listener; (c) connection reuse for the CLI itself (decision 14's deferred daemon), since the CLI's connect on every call (about 195 ms on the dev LAN, 585 ms in the real environment of 2026-10-31) is its largest gap from MCP's 9–11 ms per reused call.
    - **This answers** whether MCP or the CLI is primary (raised 2026-11-01 for Phase 8's order): the skill for shell-driven agents keeps its place ahead of MCP packaging. VISION.md's Purpose and Speed sections record it.
47. **Hosted MCP goes on the roadmap with one identity model, Entra pass-through (owner decision, 2026-11-01, from a colleague's request for Cowork-style access).** A hosted `sqlh mcp` for remote connectors (claude.ai's connect from Anthropic's cloud) is planned, not started: each caller signs in with Entra ID, sqlh exchanges the caller's token for one scoped to SQL (on-behalf-of) and logs in with it, so every query runs as that person, with their permissions and in the server's own audit. Its targets are servers that take Entra logins: SQL Server 2022 and later with Entra set up, Azure SQL Managed Instance and Azure SQL Database. The first environment is a colleague's SQL Server 2025 Standard on an Azure VM, with a Managed Instance perhaps to follow; PaaS hosting is preferred (Windows App Service, say). The plan: "Hosted MCP with Entra pass-through" (§4).
    - **Why:** the host holds no shared SQL credential, so nothing on it is worth stealing; database permissions stay the boundary person by person (VISION.md, Safety); and the driver already has a token login (`AccessToken`, read from mssql-tds 0.3.0's source, untested).
    - **Option (a), a shared read-only login per profile on the host, is the backup:** not built and not planned, but open for reconsideration if a server without Entra (SQL Server 2016 to 2019, say) needs hosting. Options (b), a login per caller stored on the host, and (d), Kerberos delegation, are not planned.
    - **For decision 46's open questions:** this answers (a) for the internet tier, and its plan builds MCP's own exe first, (b), so `sqlh.exe` never listens on a network; loopback and team-service tiers stay undecided, and the hosted server runs on an application server, which decision 1 as amended (2026-11-01) allows for MCP.
48. **The CLI is optimized for local, offline and small models; no daemon; MCP is the recommendation for frontier models (owner decision, 2026-11-01).** The CLI's audience is agents on local or offline models such as Qwen in OpenCode (agent trial 3), generally on GPUs with 24 GB of VRAM or less. When a choice trades their context or their reading for something else, theirs wins: compact output, a short `--help`, fewer calls per answer, and results that say what to do next. The user docs recommend MCP for frontier models (README, Quick start), whose context barely notices sqlh's tool definitions and which get connection reuse there.
    - **No daemon behind the CLI (decision 14's deferral stands, now with evidence; decision 46's open question (c) closed):** in agent trial 3, 47 calls across ten tasks took 1,844 s, about 39 s a call, mostly the model's own generating and reading; a connect is 195 ms on the dev LAN (about 0.5% of that) and 585 ms in the real environment of 2026-10-31 (about 1.5%). A daemon's costs (auto-start, stale processes, idle exit, pipe security, cancellation across processes, harnesses that kill it with their commands) buy too little for these agents. A script's batches, split by `GO`, already share one connect.
    - **Reopen it** only if a measurement shows the connect is a material share of real CLI turns (a fast model over a slow link, say).
49. **SQL Server 2016 SP3 and later are supported for reading, tested on a 2016 instance of the owner's (owner decision, 2026-11-01).** It replaces the "candidate 2016 floor" for the database server: real use already runs there (the owner's availability group on SQL Server 2016 SP3 Enterprise, 2026-10-31, where everything worked, `describe` after its 2016 fix). Catalog SQL must run on 2016 SP3; a feature that needs a later version says so in its output on an older server instead of failing (the planned time-zone name in `info` needs `CURRENT_TIMEZONE()`, 2019 and later); the smoke script and the live tests run against the 2016 instance as well as the 2025 test server. SQL Server 2025 stays the main validation target. The plan: "SQL Server 2016 support" (§4).
    - **Why:** without a 2016 test, a change can break the version in real use unnoticed, as `describe`'s `STRING_AGG ... WITHIN GROUP` once did.
    - **Not changed:** a Windows 10 / Windows Server 2016 floor for the machine sqlh runs on stays a candidate (VISION.md); Managed Instance and Azure SQL follow their own plan; SQL Server 2012 and 2014 are not targets.
50. **The first release is a private `0.1.0` for the owner and a few colleagues, with Windows authentication in scope (owner decision, 2026-11-01).** The repository stays private; colleagues get the signed exe from the GitHub release (with access to the repository) or as the release zip passed on. It ships what is built: the CLI, the schema commands and the local `sqlh mcp`, with Phase 6 Part 1's first slice (amended by the owner, 2026-11-01: that slice changes refusal wording agents read, so it goes in before colleagues start, and one external review covers both). It doesn't wait for the rest of Phase 6, which then gets real use; nor for hosted MCP (decision 47). Its gates are §7's checklist, which this decision completes: Windows authentication checked live, the SQL Server 2016 run (decision 49), a clean workstation, and SmartScreen and antivirus on a locked-down one. A public release (`0.2` or `1.0`) comes later, after Phase 6 Part 1 is in use and the README is reviewed for strangers; making the repository public stays the owner's separate decision. Amended by decision 91 (2026-11-23): the repository can go public at a `0.1.x`, after at least one more release. Amended by decision 96 (2026-11-26): it goes public with 0.2.0, slice 2, and no later version is named for it.
    - **The Windows authentication check (owner's suggestion):** a local Windows user on the test server, with a SQL Server login for it (`CREATE LOGIN [SERVER\user] FROM WINDOWS`, the owner's to make). The workstation isn't in a domain with the server, so its SSPI logs in by NTLM with that user's credentials: the owner starts a shell with them (`runas /netonly /user:SERVER\user`, which asks for the password itself), and sqlh runs there with a Windows-auth profile. Nothing stores or passes the password but Windows. It checks sqlh's SSPI path end to end (`SUSER_SNAME()` and `auth_scheme` in `sys.dm_exec_connections` show who and how) and its errors (a refused login, a user without a SQL login). Kerberos (an SPN, a domain) stays unchecked until a domain environment is at hand, the owner's real one say; that gap is recorded with the result.
51. **Phase 6 Part 1 is built in thin slices, each released and used before the next; Part 2 waits for real use (owner decision, 2026-11-01).** Decisions 22–36 stand; this sets their order. Part 1's slices, in order (command-categories.md §6; its §10 numbers the live checks):
    1. refusal wording and the five small items (decisions 23, 30): no live check needed;
    2. `activity`, `errorlog` and `info`'s view permissions (decision 28): after live check 10;
    3. read list round 2 and the exact-shape reads (decision 26): after live checks 2 and 4;
    4. lock-holding SQL and SNAPSHOT as a run setting (decision 24): after the owner approves live check 3;
    5. the client-side pause and showplan hygiene (decisions 27, 29): after live checks 5 and 6;
    6. linked-server reads (decision 25): last, since live check 1, the MSDTC question, is blocked.

    Each slice ships in a release of its own (decision 50; versioned by the semver rule, decision 96: slice 2 is 0.2.0) and gets real use before the next starts. **Part 2** (decisions 22, 31–36: the kinds, the deny tier, `kill`, per-database runs, trusted entries; no `execute` tool) is built once the colleagues' use of `0.1.x` shows which changes agents need to make. **Why:** the decisions were all taken on one day, before any code, and the rules are lexical, so each family is better found in use than in review (R48 was such a gap); small models gain most from slices 1 and 2 (decision 48), which need the least.
52. **External review goes by risk; small follow-ups fold into the next round (owner decision, 2026-11-01).**
    - **Reviewed externally, before it is built on:** the guard and run policy (the classifier, allowlists, the wrapper, write policy); the output contract (DESIGN.md §5's formats, exit codes, status lines); credentials and secrets (Credential Manager, `Secret`, profiles, connect hints); the engine, cancellation and the MCP protocol; driver upgrades; the release and signing workflows.
    - **Self-verified, then covered by the next external review's range, with no round of its own:** a P3 fix that touches only tests or docs, a docs-only change, a test-only change. Each is checked with `scripts/check.ps1` (and `-Live` where it touches a live test) and recorded in this plan with what it fixes and how it was checked; the next brief lists them so the reviewer sees every line, later and batched.
    - **Before any release** (`0.1.0`, `0.2.0` and each after): every change since the last external review is covered by one.
    - **Why:** the reviews find real defects (R35–R39, R42, R48, R49), but R50, a one-line test fix, took a full brief, review and verification round, as R46's and R47's verifications did for little.

53. **The profiles file moves out of `%APPDATA%`, to `%USERPROFILE%\.sqlh\profiles.toml` (owner decision, 2026-11-01).** Windows redirects `%APPDATA%` for packaged apps, so a user's shell and an agent started by the Claude desktop app could read different files, and the failure, `no profile named`, says nothing about why (§4, The profiles file under a packaged app). The alternative, keeping the path and explaining it in the error, would have left every colleague needing `SQLH_CONFIG`. Runs bring in what's under `%APPDATA%`, merging two copies without losing a profile (DESIGN.md §8); passwords stay in Credential Manager, unchanged.
54. **No test instance under an accent-insensitive server collation for `0.1.0` (owner decision, 2026-11-02).** One scratch database showed that the parser's keywords don't follow a database's collation; whether they follow an instance's is unmeasured (§4, Another collation, measured, open item (i)). It stays documented in DESIGN.md §7, and the guard is measured only on instances of `SQL_Latin1_General_CP1_CI_AS`. The alternative was installing such an instance and rerunning the sweeps before release. To reopen if a server sqlh is used on has an `_AI` collation (`SELECT SERVERPROPERTY('Collation')`), where `SET N<U+00D3>EXEC ON` and the like might be real statements the guard passes.
55. **Read-only runs refuse `AI_GENERATE_EMBEDDINGS` (owner decision, 2026-11-02).** Asked "should read-only runs refuse AI_GENERATE_EMBEDDINGS?", the owner's words: "yes, refuse it". Refused the way `OPENROWSET` is, in read-only runs and so in every `sqlh mcp` call; `--allow-write` lifts it where a profile allows that, and the `remote` kind (decision 22) can grant it later. Why: on SQL Server 2025 `AI_GENERATE_EMBEDDINGS(... USE MODEL m)` is a function, so the guard read it as a read, and with an external model defined it sends its text to an outside endpoint, as `sp_invoke_external_rest_endpoint`, already refused, does; an agent misled by what it read could send what it reads off the server, and no rollback calls that back. The cost: a read-only or `sqlh mcp` query that embeds at query time. The alternative was keeping it allowed, given that only a DBA can define a model. A view or a user function over it stays invisible, as for the others (DESIGN.md §7).

56. **Read-only runs refuse a call of `sys.fn_ledger_retrieve_digests_from_url` (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Asked "should `sys.fn_ledger_retrieve_digests_from_url` be refused now?", with refusing it by name like `OPENROWSET` recommended, and `fn_get_audit_file_v2` and `fn_full_dblog` going to decision 25's D4b, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". Refused in read-only runs and so in every `sqlh mcp` call; `--allow-write` lifts it where a profile allows that, and the `remote` kind (decision 22) can grant it later. Why: its definition reads from the URL its one argument names, so a query can put what it read in that URL, in a variable as well, with no model or anything else a DBA set up, decision 55's harm with less in its way; leaving it to D4b would have shipped it in 0.1.0. The cost: a read of ledger digests from a URL, and a user's function of that name. The evidence is its definition; it was never called.

57. **Read-only runs refuse `USE MODEL` inside a call (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Asked what to do about `VECTOR_DISTANCE(N'x' USE MODEL m)`, which passed the guard and, given as a string to `sys.dm_exec_describe_first_result_set`, ended its session on the 2025 test server with a stack dump, with refusing `USE MODEL` inside parentheses recommended and no test of it on the server, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". Refused in read-only runs and so in every `sqlh mcp` call; `--allow-write` lifts it where a profile allows that. Why: `AI_GENERATE_EMBEDDINGS`, refused by decision 55, was its one read-only use, so it costs nothing that runs, and it keeps a read-only agent from a shape that faulted the server. Only inside parentheses, since `USE model`, the statement, switches to the `model` database. The alternative, running the shape directly to learn whether it faults the server too, was not taken, to spare the server more dumps. The string route through the describe function stays open.

58. **`$(var)` keeps its fallback to environment variables in 0.1.0 (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Asked whether `$(var)` should keep falling back to the environment variable of that name, with keeping it for the first release recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". As built: a name `-v` doesn't set comes from the environment, as in `sqlcmd` (DESIGN.md §4.3), except `SQLCMDPASSWORD`, which is exit 3; `sqlh mcp` has no fallback, and `-x` turns substitution off. Why: it is what `sqlcmd` users and scripts expect, and the first release's users have a shell, which reads the environment anyway; the fallback matters only where a model can run sqlh but can't read the environment (an allowlisted harness, a prompt injection). Not taken, for when the tool leaves the owner's colleagues: allowing only `-v` and `SQLCMD*` names (a script using `$(USERNAME)` gets exit 3 until given `-v`), or a flag or profile key that turns the fallback off.

59. **Read-only runs refuse a `RAISERROR` that doesn't quote its message (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Asked whether a numbered `RAISERROR` should be refused, first with keeping it recommended; the owner asked whether a very high number wouldn't have bigger effects, which led to SQL Server Agent's alerts, and asked about severity 20 and above, which `WITH LOG`'s refusal already keeps from read-only runs (error 2754 without it, measured on both servers); with refusing any first argument but a quoted string then recommended, the owner's words: "i'll go with your recommendation". Refused in read-only runs and so in every `sqlh mcp` call; `--allow-write` lifts it where a profile allows that. Why: a message number can name one of the 669 event-logged messages, raisable by any login at severity 18 or below, whose log entry no rollback undoes and which an Agent alert on its number or severity turns into an email, a page or a job; a variable can hold a number. The cost: `RAISERROR(@msg, 16, 1)`, rewritten as `RAISERROR('%s', 16, 1, @msg)`, which the refusal shows, and a script raising a message added with `sp_addmessage`. The alternative was keeping it, as documented, with the harm taken as a line in a log.

60. **A temp table written another way stays refused as a write target in read-only runs (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Asked whether `[#t]`, `"#t"`, `tempdb..#t` and `tempdb.dbo.#t`, which the servers read as the session's table, should count as scratch targets as `#t` does, with keeping the refusal recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". As built (DESIGN.md §7): a write through one is refused with `INSERT [#t] isn't read as a temp table: only the bare name is` and `Write it as #t` first. Why: agents rarely write it so, the advice gets them past in one retry even where `--allow-write` can't (a `deny` profile, `sqlh mcp`), and the rule stays one bare token's, with no qualifier to tell apart from one that points elsewhere. The alternative, allowing the four spellings, was a few lines more in the rule and a wider way past it.

61. **Read-only runs refuse a temp table's name where it can only be an alias or a CTE's name (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Asked whether a read-only run should refuse a write through an alias or CTE named like a temp table (`WITH #c AS (SELECT * FROM dbo.T) DELETE FROM #c`, `UPDATE #o ... FROM dbo.Orders AS #o`), documented in DESIGN.md §7 but never asked, with refusing a `#` name where it can only be an alias or a CTE's name recommended, measured first, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". Refused in read-only runs and so in every `sqlh mcp` call, whether or not the statement writes; `--allow-write` lifts it. Why: the write reached the real table, rolled back but holding its exclusive locks until then, which can block production for the run's timeout, and firing its triggers; no agent writes these shapes by accident, so they are what getting past the guard looks like. Measured first, the servers bind quoted and collation-equal names too (`[#o ]`, `[<joiner>#o]`, `[<U+FF03>o]`), so the rule reads those as well (DESIGN.md §7). The cost: a column's alias spelled with `#`, `EXEC sp_help #t` unquoted, and a few percent of the guard's time on text dense with temp names (§4). The alternative was leaving it documented, relying on the rollback.

62. **A listed procedure is taken only in its lowercase spelling, with `sys` and `dbo` (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Asked whether allowlisted names should have to be written in their exact case, with that recommended unmeasured, the owner chose "Measure it first in a case-sensitive scratch database"; measured on both servers (§4, open item (ii)), a user's `SP_WHO`, `SYS.sp_who` and the like ran in a read-only run. Asked then, the owner wanted to know whether the check could be case-sensitive only when the target is; it can't well, since the guard runs before connecting and a script's `USE` or an `sqlh mcp` call's database changes the target, and with the exact spelling everywhere recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". Refused in read-only runs and so in every `sqlh mcp` call, with `Write it as <name>`; `--allow-write` lifts it. `master` stayed in any case until the 0.1.0 review's R54: **amended (owner, 2026-11-07),** asked whether to require lowercase `master` too, as R54 requests, with that recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation"; a database's name compares by the server's collation, so on a case-sensitive server `MASTER` can be another database, and `master` is now taken only in lowercase, with the same refusal. Why: in a case-sensitive database anyone who can create a procedure can have a read-only run execute it, with the caller's rights, under a listed name in another case; every listed name is lowercase, as agents copy it. The cost: `EXEC SP_WHO` and the like, refused with the spelling to use. The alternatives were accepting any case, documented, and checking the database's collation after connecting.

63. **Collations that fold ASCII stay documented, not handled, for `0.1.0` (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Asked whether the guard should handle collations under which ASCII spellings are equal (`sis.`, `[sy-s].`, `[sy:s].`, `su<U+0308>s.` for `sys.` in Frisian, Maori, Mohawk and Nordic ones), which pass decision 30(d)'s rule in its `NULL` shape, with leaving them documented recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". As DESIGN.md §7 states, unmeasured. Why: only decision 30(d)'s name match guesses how a name resolves, the others failing closed; no model types these by accident; the harm is one costly scan, not a write; and handling them would take a table of each collation's folds. The alternative was measuring each in a scratch database of its collation and widening the match to what resolves.

64. **`--allow-write` lifts decision 30(d)'s refusal, which goes on not offering it (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** A departure the slice-1 build reported and the owner hadn't ruled on: the refusal of `sys.dm_db_index_physical_stats` over every database in a scanning mode is a read-only run's rule, so with writes allowed the scan runs as written, while DESIGN.md §7 said no flag lifts it. Asked whether to keep that, with keeping it and documenting it recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". DESIGN.md §7 now says so; `allow_write_lets_the_scan_of_every_index_run` holds it. Why: `--allow-write` is the deliberate switch for a run its user takes charge of, a DBA who means the scan needs a way to run it, and an agent isn't pointed there (no advice offers the flag, a `deny` profile refuses it, `sqlh mcp` has none). The alternative was refusing it under `--allow-write` too, leaving no way to run it through sqlh.

65. **Decision 30(d)'s refusal keeps a sentence for each mode (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** A departure the slice-1 build reported: command-categories.md §6.10 gave only `'DETAILED'`'s sentence (`would read every page of every index in every database`), which is false of `'SAMPLED'`, so `'SAMPLED'` got `would read at least 1% of the pages of every index in every database` (DESIGN.md §7; the figure is the function's documentation, not a measurement). Asked whether to keep both, with that recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". As built. Why: agents act on what a refusal says, so it shouldn't overstate the cost. The alternative was §6.10's one sentence for both modes.

66. **Decision 30(c)'s application name follows what a run can do, not its flags (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** A departure the slice-1 build reported: `--check --allow-write`, the schema commands and `sqlh profiles test` log in as `sqlh`, since each runs in the read-only wrapper and can change nothing, whatever the flags or `write_policy` (DESIGN.md §4.3). Asked whether to keep that, with keeping it recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". As built. Why: the label tells a DBA whether a session may change things, and `sqlh (writes)` on a compile-only check or a `tables` listing would be an alarm for nothing that teaches DBAs to ignore it. The alternative was the name by flags: `sqlh (writes)` whenever `--allow-write` or `write_policy = "allow"` applies.

67. **Decision 23's wording: `DBCC IND`'s replacement keeps `(undocumented)`, and `DROP` names its statement (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Two points the slice-1 build left: the refusal of `DBCC IND` reads `Read sys.dm_db_database_page_allocations (undocumented) instead`, where command-categories.md §6.9 named the function bare; and `DROP` other than `DROP TABLE` was a bare `DROP would write`, §6.9's rule naming only `ALTER` and `CREATE` statements (its probe J33 showed the bare form). Asked whether to keep the mark and name the `DROP` statements, with both recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". The mark stays, as built; `DROP` now names its statement from `guard::DDL_STATEMENTS`, as `ALTER` and `CREATE` do (`DROP DATABASE would write`, `DROP INDEX would write`; one the list doesn't name, such as `DROP SIGNATURE`, stays bare). Why: an agent should know the function's columns may change between versions, and that it tried to drop a database rather than an index. The alternatives were §6.9's bare function name and the bare `DROP`.

68. **Decision 30(e) refuses the `OFF` forms in `sqlh mcp` too (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** A departure the slice-1 build reported: `SET AUTOPILOT OFF` and `SET IDENTITY_INSERT #t OFF` are refused with the `ON` forms (DESIGN.md §7: the rule goes by the statement). Asked whether to keep that, with keeping it recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". As built. Why: with `ON` refused, no earlier call left the state on, so `OFF` is a no-op, and reading `ON` from `OFF` would be another unreserved word to compare by the collation, where bypasses have come from; the CLI runs both. The alternative was allowing `OFF` in `sqlh mcp`.

69. **The new login after `SET AUTOPILOT ON` is left to the MCP reset test (owner decision, 2026-11-07).** Observed by hand before decision 30(e)'s refusal: through `sqlh mcp --pool 1`, the call after `SET AUTOPILOT ON` returned its rows on a new login, unexplained (command-categories.md §6). Asked whether to reproduce it now, with leaving it to command-categories.md §10 item 5 recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". Item 5 now asks for the reason too. Why: `sqlh mcp` refuses `SET AUTOPILOT` in every form, so no call reaches that state; the outcome was safe (the right rows, on a fresh session), likely the reset or its probe failing under `AUTOPILOT` and decision 19's silent retry; and item 5 decides whether the refusal can ever lift, which is where the reason matters. The alternative was a debug build that skips the refusal, to reproduce it before `0.1.0`.

70. **0.1.0 ships on mssql-tds 0.4.0, and on no later driver (owner decision, 2026-11-08, driver-0.4.0.md §7.1).** Asked whether to release 0.1.0 on 0.3.0 and take 0.4.0 on a branch afterwards (recommended: 0.1.0's gates were checked on 0.3.0, and the bump needs its own external review), the owner's words: "I have decided 0.1.0 will wait for mssql-tds 0.4.0, but that is where we will draw the line. no further upstream versions in this release." So step 2 (driver-0.4.0.md §9) is now on 0.1.0's path: the bump with the remote-pipe refusal widened for IPv6 prefixes, its external review (decision 52), and the release checks the bump touches rerun on 0.4.0 (§7). A driver released after 0.4.0 waits for the release after 0.1.0, whatever it fixes; a security fix in it would come back to the owner as a new decision. Why: the off-runtime SPN lookup and SSPI calls (U9, partly fixed) go into the first release, and a fixed line keeps the release date from following upstream. The alternative was 0.1.0 on 0.3.0, with 0.4.0 in 0.1.1.

71. **sqlh asks for vector feature version 2, the driver's default since mssql-tds 0.4.0, and sets it explicitly (owner decision, 2026-11-08, driver-0.4.0.md §7.2).** Asked which version to ask for, with version 1 recommended (so the upgrade changes no output; version 2 later as its own item), the owner's words: "I want to flow with the upstream's default". So `Session::connect` sets `VectorVersion::V2`, which is what the driver would pick anyway, so that a later default can't change how vectors arrive unseen, as decision 40 did for failover; the LOGIN7 test pins the 0x0E byte at 2, and that 0x09 (data classification) is absent. float32 vectors arrive and print as before. float16 vectors (SQL Server 2025's preview half-precision type) arrive natively once the server acknowledges version 2 (the driver also accepts an acknowledgement of 1, and float16 then comes down-level, as before; which one 2025 sends is not yet observed, since `sqlh_test` keeps `PREVIEW_FEATURES` off and the server refuses the type, error 195; observed 2026-11-14 on a throwaway VM: build 17.0.1155 sent float16 down-level to sqlh, driver-0.4.0.md §10, item 8), and the driver widens each element to f32 exactly; sqlh writes each in the shortest decimal that reads back as the same float16, DESIGN.md §5.1's shortest round-trip form at the element's own precision (`0.1`, not `0.099975586`), and a whole number whole (`65504`, not its shortest form `65500`); checked against every finite float16, and through the driver's decoder by a fake-server row of both column types. The header's type stays `vector` (the `vector(N, float16)` spelling of driver-0.4.0.md §6.6 is not taken). Why: follow upstream where it costs nothing to sqlh's contract. The alternatives were version 1 (no output change) and off (no vector feature; float32 vectors down-level too).

72. **Windows auth's SPN comes from the driver's per-hop hook, `SecurityContextProvider`, built now, for 0.1.0 (owner decision, 2026-11-08, driver-0.4.0.md §7.3 and §6.1).** Asked whether to keep sqlh's 500 ms SPN pre-lookup before the connect, with keeping it recommended and the hook recorded as the replacement after 0.1.0, the owner's words: "build the long term replacement now. as I said I want to flow with the 0.4.0's defaults, this is in the spirit of that ask". So the pre-lookup (`spn_if_lookup_is_slow`, `host_and_spn`, the `-K ReadOnly` skip) is replaced by a provider the driver calls for each login hop with the host and port it dials: SQL Browser's port for a named instance, and each routed host. The lookup keeps its 500 ms bound and its cap (R53), and closes R52's two exclusions. The cost, accepted with it: the lookup moves inside the login deadline, after TCP, prelogin and TLS, where DESIGN.md §4.2 promised it before `-l`'s limits; and it is new authentication code, so it goes to the external review with the driver upgrade (decision 52). The alternative was keeping the pre-lookup for 0.1.0 and the hook after it.

73. **The local-pipe check takes this computer's name from the driver (owner decision, 2026-11-08, driver-0.4.0.md §7.4).** Asked whether `check_server` should compare `np:<name>` with the driver's `this_computer_name()` (public since mssql-tds 0.4.0: the DNS host name, `GetComputerNameExW`, which the driver itself compares to decide a pipe is local) instead of the `COMPUTERNAME` environment variable, with the driver's recommended, the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". Built: `db::this_computer`, the driver's name, with `COMPUTERNAME` only when the driver has none. Why: the refusal exists to match what the driver will open; with `COMPUTERNAME`, an environment naming another host let `np:<that host>` through to the driver's remote pipe open (U1), while the driver's own name can't be moved by the environment. The cost: on a machine whose DNS name is longer than 15 characters, `np:<NetBIOS name>` is now refused (with the TCP spelling); it went over SMB to this machine. The alternative was keeping `COMPUTERNAME`.

74. **mssql-tds 0.4.0's other new features wait for after 0.1.0, as candidates (owner decision, 2026-11-08, driver-0.4.0.md §7.5).** Asked whether to record them as candidates after 0.1.0, with that recommended (none is needed for the release, and decision 70 holds 0.1.0 to the upgrade itself), the owner's words: "yes, i'll go with your recommendation". The candidates, each a feature of its own with its own design, review and, for the first two, output changes: (a) the connect-stage spans (driver-0.4.0.md §6.2) for `--verbose`, `sqlh profiles test` and `db::phases`, which would replace the debug texts `db::phases` matches; a tracing subscriber only if it measures no larger than the log path, and enabling only `mssql_tds::connect` spans (AGENTS.md, Driver logging); (b) sensitivity labels for a DBA, by the catalog (`sys.sensitivity_classifications`, SQL Server 2019 and later), not the driver's data-classification token (§6.5); (c) refusing a login to a cached address or a routed host sqlh didn't name (§6.3), only if decisions 42 and 43 are to become enforceable. The first of §7.5's list, the per-hop SPN hook, was built for 0.1.0 by decision 72.
75. **AppVeyor runs the release's own exe and tests against its SQL Servers; GitHub builds everything; AppVeyor holds a read-only GitHub token (owner decisions, 2026-11-08, §4, AppVeyor).** The owner offered an AppVeyor account for integration tests of other SQL editions, "not triggered every time" but for release certification. Asked about a draft that compiled on AppVeyor's VM, the owner's words: "compile should stay on the GH runner. I wanted to use the AppVeyor VM for integration/release tests after the exe is built." So `release.yml`'s build job also builds the live suite's test binary and keeps it as a workflow artifact, never a release asset; AppVeyor downloads it and the signed exe (the `sqlh-<version>` artifact `sign` uploads) from that commit's release run, checks the exe's signature and `SHA256SUMS.txt`, and runs the live tests against each instance, only for a tag or a build started by hand. The repository is private, and GitHub's signed download links expire in minutes, so AppVeyor needs a token. Asked, with recommended a fine-grained token for `sequeltom/sqlh` alone with Actions: read only and an expiry, kept as an AppVeyor encrypted variable and made by the owner (never passing through an agent), the owner's words: "yes, go with the read-only token". It can download workflow artifacts, and read workflow runs and their logs (Actions: read is broader than artifacts alone; corrected by the R59-R61 review), but not change code or releases, nor read the values of GitHub's stored secrets. Draft releases aren't readable with it (GitHub lists them only to accounts with push access), which is why the exe comes from the workflow artifact.
76. **SQL Server 2025 Standard Developer stands in for Standard (owner decision, 2026-11-13).** Asked about §7's open gates, the owner: "we can skip #5 - I trust Standard Developer to be equal to Standard." So §7's Standard line is closed by the test server's Standard Developer Edition (SQL auth throughout the live suite; Windows auth by NTLM through a local user, 2026-11-01, §4, Carried over). The Hard constraints' target, SQL Server 2025 Standard, is unchanged.

77. **`profiles add`'s connection test times itself, from after the save (owner decision, 2026-11-14).** Its status line counted from the process's start, so the time spent typing the password was in it (4782 ms for a 211 ms connect, the Sandbox check; §4, Carried over). Offered A, time from after the prompt (recommended), or B, leave it since only a person runs `profiles add`, the owner: "yes, go with A". Why: every other status line gives the run's own time, and a person reading the old figure could blame the server or the network. It also leaves out a wait for the profiles lock. In 0.1.0.

78. **Kerberos in a domain is tested before 0.1.0's tag (owner decision, 2026-11-14).** Asked whether to ship the private 0.1.0 with LocalDB, a routed hop and Kerberos recorded as untested (A, recommended: the colleagues' first Windows-auth connections as the Kerberos test) or to hold the tag until Kerberos is tried in a domain (B), the owner: "B - let's make a domain joined VM. we could build a domain controller in Azure or" on the owner's own hardware. Why: Windows authentication is in 0.1.0's scope (decision 70), and colleagues on domain-joined machines will mostly use Kerberos, which no run has reached (NTLM only: AppVeyor's local account, the owner's local Windows user). So §7's mssql-tds line stays open until a domain-joined client connects to a domain-joined SQL Server by Kerberos (`auth_scheme` `KERBEROS`). Where the domain is built is the next decision. *Built in Azure, beside the real-network VM (§7, the mssql-tds line).*

79. **A name's SPN comes from its forward lookup's canonical name, not the driver's reverse lookup (owner decision, 2026-11-14).** The domain check (decision 78) found a login by short name was NTLM where Microsoft's `System.Data.SqlClient` on the same machine got Kerberos: the driver canonicalizes a short name by forward then reverse lookup, and the PTR record named Azure's host, not the domain's (upstream-issues.md U13). Offered A, fix it in sqlh's own SPN provider now (recommended), or B, ship with "connect by the full name for Kerberos" documented, the owner: "A - fix it in sqlh now". So `db::spn::canonical_name` gives a name, short or dotted, its forward lookup's canonical name (`getaddrinfo` with `AI_CANONNAME`, through `dns-lookup`, already in the tree), which also takes an alias (a CNAME) to its host, and keeps the driver's reverse rule for an address; the 500 ms budget, the cap of 4 and the loopback rule are decision 72's, unchanged. Why: people connect by short name, and where reverse DNS disagrees with the AD name they would silently get NTLM, which fails where NTLM is restricted and can't be delegated to a linked server. It touches authentication, so it goes to external review before the tag (decision 52).

80. **Changes reach `main` through pull requests (owner decision, 2026-11-14).** PR #1 (the owner's public-release preparation: Dependabot, actions pinned to commit SHAs, `SECURITY.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md`, generic wording in the records) protected `main`: a PR whose `check` CI passes, on a branch up to date with `main`, admins included, no force pushes; and a ruleset makes `v*` tags immutable. Offered A, an agent commits on a branch, pushes it and opens a PR when asked, and the owner merges or says to (recommended), or B, a rule letting the agent push `main` directly, the owner: "A - open the PR with the AGENTS update". Why: it keeps the new protection whole, at the cost of a CI wait per change; with no approvals required, a merge stays the owner's quick call. AGENTS.md's remote paragraph says so. The public-readiness check that preceded it (2026-11-14): a scan of all 352 commits' content and messages, and the tree, found none of the private names, addresses, the subscription or tenant, tokens or keys; the commits' author email is the owner's own, to weigh before going public; private vulnerability reporting and secret scanning aren't available while the repository is private, to be enabled when it goes public.

81. **0.1.0 ships without the managed-laptop check, LocalDB or a routed hop (owner decision, 2026-11-13 and 2026-11-14).** Of §7's open items, three can't be done with what is at hand. The managed laptop (corporate antivirus or EDR, AppLocker or WDAC, on a colleague's machine): asked for 0.1.0's remaining checks, the owner: "#1 can wait for the public release" (2026-11-13). LocalDB (driver-0.4.0.md §8 item 13; not installed anywhere at hand) and a routed hop (an Always On read-only route, which needs a second SQL Server and a cluster): asked whether the checklist's driver line could close with both left untested, the owner: "yes, prepare the PR closing the §7 lines" (2026-11-14). Kerberos, the third of decision 78's untested items, was tested instead (decisions 78 and 79). So the SmartScreen line and the driver line close for 0.1.0, each saying what stays untested. Why: 0.1.0 is private, for the owner and a few colleagues (decision 50), who can report what these would have found; the managed laptop is the first of the public release's checks. Amended by decision 98 (2026-11-27): the repository went public before the managed-laptop check, which is now a check after the release.

82. **The name reads as "SQL for harnesses" (owner decision, 2026-11-14).** The owner noted the docs no longer explained the "h" in sqlh, and asked whether the tool is a SQL harness or a SQL tool for harnesses. A tool for harnesses: a harness (Claude Code, OpenCode) is the runtime that drives the model and calls tools, which sqlh is one of. Offered "SQL for harnesses" as a one-place origin note, with or without a line on the guardrails "harnessing" what an agent does with SQL, the owner: "SQL for harnesses, without the restraint line". So the README's first sentence says "(sqlh: SQL for harnesses)", and DESIGN.md's Name entry says why; "SQL harness" isn't used as a name, since it reads as the project the 2026-10-12 rename avoided. `--help` and the MCP descriptions are unchanged: models read them, and they need the tool's name, not its origin.
83. **`activity` marks no session as `sqlh mcp`'s own, and the engine learns no session ids (owner decision, 2026-11-21; amends decision 28).** Decision 28 had `activity` hide that `sqlh mcp`'s idle pooled connections and mark its busy ones as its own, which needs each pooled connection's session id: the driver exposes none (no accessor, and LOGIN7's ClientPID is always 0), so it meant a `SELECT @@SPID` in each new connection's setup and a registry in the engine. The slice 2 design review (2026-11-21) found the hiding rule does nothing on its own: `put_back` rolls an idle connection back, so it has no request and no open transaction, and `activity`'s row rule (a user session with a request or an open transaction, or one another shown session is blocked by) already leaves it out, and shows it, as its blocker, when it holds the shared database lock something waits on. That left only the mark. Offered dropping it (recommended: every `sqlh mcp` session already shows `program` `sqlh mcp` and `host` the machine), a `CONTEXT_INFO` token in `sqlh mcp`'s session setup, the engine registry as decided, or a guess in SQL from program, host and login, the owner: "Drop own". So `activity` has no `own` column, the engine and the session setup are unchanged, and an agent tells a `sqlh mcp` session by its `program` and `host`; what it can't tell apart is its own server's busy session from another `sqlh mcp`'s on the same machine and login.
84. **`errorlog --since` reads back across the log's archives (owner decision, 2026-11-21; adds to decision 28).** Each restart and each `sp_cycle_errorlog` starts a new archive, so archive 0 often begins hours ago: on `test` it began at the last restart, and `--since 7d` from one archive would have returned about 10 hours that read as complete, while the lead-up to a restart is always in archive 1 (slice 2 design review, 2026-11-21). Offered (a) reading as many archives as the window needs (recommended), (b) one archive with a note saying where it starts, or (c) one archive, documented, the owner: "a". So with `--since`, `errorlog` reads archive 0, then 1 and on, newest first into the one list, stopping after the first archive whose oldest line is at or before the window's start, and reads only archives `sp_enumerrorlogs` lists (S1, the exit-10 trap, stays closed); without it, archive 0 or the one `--archive N` names; `--since` and `--archive` together are a usage error (exit 3), since each picks what is read. A wide window on a large log reads longer, bounded by `-t`.
